  • (d) that the Bill be circulated for the purpose of eliciting opinion thereon, within such period as may be specified in the motion.
  • (2) The “Second stage” consists of clause-by-clause consideration of the Bill as introduced. If the Bill is referred to the Select Committee or to the Joint Committee, the report will be submitted by the Select Committee /Joint Committee to the House. The said report will be laid on table of the House and the Bill will be considered clause-by-clause.
  • (3) The “Third stage” consists of discussion on the motion that the Bill be passed. If the motion is passed, the Bill will be considered as passed by the Legislative Assembly.
  • (4) After Bill has been passed by the Legislative Assembly, the Speaker will sign a certificate at the foot of the Bill in the following form:- “This Bill was passed by the Legislative Assembly of the State of Maharashtra on the ……day of ………20… Speaker.”.
  • (5) The certified Bill will be transmitted to the Secretary of the Legislative Council for the consideration of the Council. Passing of Bill by Council.
  • 6.14. (1) The copies of the Bill passed by the Legislative Assembly will be laid on the table of the Legislative Council.
  • (2) After a Bill has been laid on the table of the Assembly, it is not necessary to make a motion for leave to introduce the same in the Council.
  • (3) In Legislative Council, the “First stage” consists of discussion on the Bill and on any one of the motion specified in para 6.13 (1).
  • (4) The “Second Stage” consists of the Bill will be considered clause-by-clause by the Legislative Council.
  • (5) The “Third stage” consists of discussion on the motion that the Bill be passed. If the motion is passed, the Bill will be considered as passed by the Legislative Council.
  • (6) After passing of the Bill by Council (originating in Assembly), it is considered, the Bill has been passed by both Houses.
  • (7) After Bill has been passed by the Legislative Assembly and Legislative Council, if the Bill is the Legislative Assembly Bill, the Speaker will sign at the foot of the Bill; and if the Bill is the Legislative Council Bill, the Chairman will sign at the foot of the Bill. H 2316-8a Procedure in case of Bill originating in Council (Rule 149 of MLA Rules).
  • 6.15 (1) Where the Bill is introduced in the Council, the copy of the Bill passed by the Council will be transmitted to the Assembly and will be laid on the table of the Assembly.
  • (2) After a Bill has been laid on the table of the Assembly, it is not necessary to make a motion for leave to introduce the same in the Assembly.
  • (3) At any time after copies of a Bill have been laid on the Table, the Minister in charge of Bill will give notice of his intention to move the Bill and thereupon the provisions of rules 119 to 157 of Assembly Rules will be applicable for passing of Bill.
  • (4) After passing of the Bill by Assembly (originating in Council), it is considered, the Bill has been passed by both Houses.
  • (5) While each Bill has to undergo the three readings or stages mentioned above, some categories of Bills such as Government Bills, Money Bill and Financial Bills and private members' Bills, (Non-official Bill) have special procedural aspects and are, therefore, treated separately. Amendments in Bill 134/130.
  • 6.16 (1) Any member may at the time of consideration of Bill move an amendment to the Bill.
  • (2) The Speaker have power to select the new clauses or amendments to be proposed, and may, if he thinks fit, call upon any member who has given notice of an amendments to give such explanation of the object of the amendments as may enable him to form a judgment upon it.
  • (3) Amendments are considered in the order of the clauses to which they relate.
  • (4) If the amendment is accepted, it becomes part of the Bill.
  • (5) The detail procedure of passing of Bill with or without amendments is provided in rules 136 to 151 of Assembly Rules and in rules 132 to 146 in the Council Rules. Withdrawal of amendments Rule 135/131.
  • 6.17. An amendment moved to the Bill may be withdrawn, by leave of the House, on the request of the member moving it.

Deemed passed Bill by Both Houses Article 197.

  • 6.18. Article 197 of the Constitution provides the procedure of deemed passing Bill by Houses of the State Legislature.

“197. Restriction on powers of Legislative Council as to

Bills other than Money Bills.- (1) If after a Bill has been passed by the Legislative Assembly of a State having a Legislative Council and transmitted to the Legislative Council—

  • (a) the Bill is rejected by the Council; or
  • (b) more than three months elapse from the date on which the Bill is laid before the Council without the Bill being passed by it; or
  • (c) the Bill is passed by the Council with amendments to which the Legislative Assembly does not agree, the Legislative Assembly may, subject to the rules regulating its procedure, pass the Bill again in the same or in any subsequent session with or without such amendments, if any, as have been made, suggested or agreed to by the Legislative Council and then transmit the Bill as so passed to the Legislative Council.
  • (2) If after a Bill has been so passed for the second time by the Legislative Assembly and transmitted to the Legislative Council—
  • (a) the Bill is rejected by the Council; or
  • (b) more than one month elapses from the date on which the Bill is laid before the Council without the Bill being passed by it; or
  • (c) the Bill is passed by the Council with amendments to which the Legislative Assembly does not agree, the Bill shall be deemed to have been passed by the Houses of the Legislature of the State in the form in which it was passed by the Legislative Assembly for the second time with such amendments, if any, as have been made or suggested by the Legislative Council and agreed to by the Legislative Assembly.
  • (3) Nothing in this article shall apply to a Money Bill.”.
  • 6.18.1. The detail procedure of deemed passing of Bill, consideration of amendments, rejection of Bill by House is provided in rules 141 to 147 of the Assembly Rules.
  • 6.18.2. The detail procedure of consideration of amendments, return of Bill by Assembly, procedure in case Bill passed for second time by Assembly, is provided in rules 137 to 142 of the Council Rules.

Procedure in case of Money Bill, Article 198 of Constitution.

  • 6.19. Article 198 of the Constitution provides the procedure of passing of Money Bill.

“198. Special procedure in respect of Money Bills. (1) A Money

Bill shall not be introduced in a Legislative Council.

  • (2) After a Money Bill has been passed by the Legislative Assembly of a State having a Legislative Council, it shall be transmitted to the Legislative Council for its recommendations, and the Legislative Council shall within a period of fourteen days from the date of its receipt of the Bill return the Bill to the Legislative Assembly with its recommendations, and the Legislative Assembly may thereupon either accept or reject all or any of the recommendations of the Legislative Council.
  • (3) If the Legislative Assembly accepts any of the recommendations of the Legislative Council, the Money Bill shall be deemed to have been passed by both Houses with the amendments recommended by the Legislative Council and accepted by the Legislative Assembly.
  • (4) If the Legislative Assembly does not accept any of the recommendations of the Legislative Council, the Money Bill shall be deemed to have been passed by both Houses in the form in which it was passed by the Legislative Assembly without any of the amendments recommended by the Legislative Council.
  • (5) If a Money Bill passed by the Legislative Assembly and transmitted to the Legislative Council for its recommendations is not returned to the Legislative Assembly within the said period of fourteen days, it shall be deemed to have been passed by both Houses at the expiration of the said period in the form in which it was passed by the Legislative Assembly.”. Procedure in case of Money Bill. Rule 148 of Assembly Rules.
  • 6.20. The detail procedure of passing of Money Bill is provided in rule 148 of the Maharashtra Assembly Rules. “148.Procedure in case of Money Bills. (1) After a Money Bill has been passed by the Assembly, the Speaker shall sign a certificate at the foot of the Bill in the following form :—

“This Bill was by the Legislative Assembly of the State of Maharashtra on the............................. day ......................... 20 . I certify that this is a Money Bill. Speaker.”

  • (2) The Bill so certified shall be transmitted to the Secretary of the Council for the recommendations of the Council.
  • (3) If any recommendations are made by the Council in the Bill and the Bill is returned to the Assembly with a message asking for the acceptance of the recommendations by the Assembly, copies of the message so received shall be send to all members and copies of the Bill with the recommendations shall be laid on the Assembly table.
  • (4) The member-in-charge may then, after giving three days’ notice, move that the recommendations made by the Council be taken into consideration. If the motion is carried, the Speaker shall put to the Assembly, the recommendations as amendments to the Bill in such manner as he thinks most convenient for their consideration. If the motion is lost or if the Assembly does not accept any of the recommendations, the Bill shall be deemed to have been passed by the Assembly and the Council in the form in which it was originally passed by the Assembly.
  • (5) If the Assembly accepts any amendment or amendments as recommended by the Council, the Bill shall be deemed to have been passed by both the Houses with the amendment or amendments recommended by the Council and accepted by the Assembly.
  • (6) After a Money Bill is deemed to have been passed by the Assembly and the Council, the Speaker shall sign a certificate at the foot of the Bill that it is a Money Bill and the Secretary shall then send a copy of the Bill signed by the Speaker for submission to the Governor through the Secretary to the Government of Maharashtra, Law and Judiciary Department.”. Procedure in the case of Money Bills passed by Assembly, rule 143 of Council.
  • 6.21. “143. Procedure in the case of Money Bills passed by the Assembly.- (1) Copies of every Money Bill passed by the Assembly and transmitted to the Council shall be laid on the table of the Council. The date of the receipt of the Money Bill by the Council shall be reported to the Secretary of the Assembly.
  • (2) At any time after the Money Bill is laid on the table of the Council under sub-rule (1) of this rule, any Minister may, after giving three days’ notice, move that the Money Bill as passed by the Assembly be taken into consideration. On such motion the general principles of the Bill may be discussed, but the detailed provisions shall not be discussed.
  • (3) If the motion is carried, the Chairman shall submit the Bill to the Council clause-by-clause. At this stage amendments to be recommended to the Assembly may be moved to the Bill. The amendments shall be considered in the order of the clauses to which they relate : Provided that with the permission of the Chairman amendments may be taken in any other order.
  • (4) If the motion is lost, the Bill shall be deemed to have been returned to the Assembly without any recommendations.
  • (5) If the Council has no recommendations to make, the Bill shall be returned to the Assembly with a message to that effect.
  • (6) If the Council has recommendations to make, the Bill shall be returned to the Assembly with a message asking for the acceptance by the Assembly of the recommendations made by the Council.
  • (7) On a Bill being introduced in the Council or at any subsequent stage, if an objection is taken that a Bill is a Money Bill within the meaning of Article 199 of the Constitution and should not be proceeded with in the Council, the Chairman shall if he holds the objection valid, direct that further proceedings in connection with the Bill be terminated.”. Withdrawal of Bill from House Rule 154/149 MLA/ MLC Rules.
  • 6.22. (1) A Bill can be withdrawn at any stage with the leave of the House.
  • (2) When the Originating Department decides to withdraw a Bill from either of the House of State Legislature, it will take the approval of the Cabinet for such withdrawal. (3)The Originating Department will prepare the motion in consultation with the Law and Judiciary Department and will send such motion to the Secretary, Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat under the signature of the concerned Minister, for withdrawal of such Bill.

Procedure when Bill referred to Select Committee. Rule 126 of MLA Rules/122ML C Rules.

  • 6.23 (1) In case of Assembly Bill, the provisions of sub-rules (2) and (3) of rule 194 and of rules 195 and 196 will apply when any Bill is referred to a Select Committee. The Minister-in-charge of the Bill is ex-officio member of the Select Committee. The Committee consists of not more than fifteen members including ex-officio member.
  • (2) In case of Council Bill, the provisions of sub-rules (2) and (3) of rule 189 and of rules 190 and 191 will apply when any Bill is referred to a Select Committee. The Minister-in-charge of the Bill is ex-officio member of the Select Committee. The Committee consists of not more than ten members including ex-officio member.
  • 6.23.1 The Committee may hear expert evidence and representatives of special interest concerned with the matter of Bill referred to it.

6.23.2

. A Select Committee have power to make such amendments in the Bill referred to it as are relevant to the subject-matter and within the scope of the Bill.

6.23.3

. The Minister-in-charge of the Bill presents the report of Select Committee in the House. The Minister-in-charge may make brief explanatory remarks about the changes made in the Bill by the Select Committee. The Minister-in-charge moves the report for consideration of the House.

6.23.4

. The Select Committee report of the Bill shall be published in the Official Gazette, by the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.

Procedure when Bill referred to Joint Committee.

  • 6.24. The Bill introduced in either of the House may be referred to the Joint Committee of both Houses, if a motion is passed in the House.

6.24.1

. The motion to refer the Bill to the Joint Committee is moved by the Minister-in-charge of the Bill. The strength of the Joint Committee is decided by the Member-in-charge.

6.24.2

. The Minister-in-charge of the Department concerned is the exofficio member of the Committee. After the Committee is constituted its Chairman is nominated by the Speaker/Chairman. He is usually the Minister-in-charge of the Bill. These details have to be incorporated in the body of the motion in the forms at Annexures XIX (6).

H 2316-9

6.24.3

. The motion is passed by one House, a concurrence motion in the form at Annexure XX (6), has to be moved by the Minister-incharge in the other House. On the passing of the concurrence motion as above, another motion is moved by the Minister-incharge mentioning the names of the members of that House, who will serve on the Joint Committee.

6.24.4

. The number of members of the Assembly nominated on such Committee shall be not less than two-thirds of the total number of members of such Committee excluding the Minister-in-charge and members of the Council nominated on such Committee shall be not less than one-third of the total number of members of such Committee. The detail provisions are made in the Rules 198 and 199 of the Assembly Rules and Rules 193 and 194 of the Council Rules, respectively.

6.24.5

. A final motion is then made in the original House for constituting the Joint Committee mentioning the names of members of the House who will serve on the Committee (vide Annexure XX (6)).

6.24.6

. The Administrative Department may take the help of the officers of the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat and the Parliamentary Affairs Department, for finalizing the procedure.

6.24.7

. The Joint Committee have power to make such amendments in the Bill referred to it as are relevant to the subject-matter and within the scope of the Bill.

6.24.8

. All matters connected with the working of the Joint Committee are looked after by the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.

6.24.9

. In case of Assembly Bill, rules 129 to 133 of the Assembly Rules will be applicable and in case of Council Bill, rules 127 to 129 of the Councils Rules will be applicable, for consideration of Bill by the Joint Committee and presentation of Report to the House.

Joint Committee procedure.

  • 6.25 (1) The Joint Committee decides the procedure to consider the Bill.

As per the practice followed, the Committee decides,-

  • (a) to publish the Bill for eliciting opinion,
  • (b) to call suggestions from general public, MLA, MLC, MP of the State on Bill,
  • (c) to take views of the experts on the subject of Bill/other persons as committee decides in the meeting,
  • (d) to visit institutions, offices, other States, etc., to consider their views on Bill.
  • (2) The Committee may hear expert evidence and representatives of special interest concerned with the matter referred to it.
  • (3) The Bill (Marathi copy) will be read clause-by-clause in meeting of the Committee. The members of the Committee may suggest amendments to clauses of Bill. If the Committee accepts the suggestions, the amendments are incorporated in the Bill. The detail minutes of the meeting are prepared by the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.
  • (4) The Secretary, Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat will send minutes of every meeting to the Law and Judiciary Department and to the concerned Administrative Department of the Bill.
  • (5) The Law and Judiciary Department will prepare the List of draft amendments (see Annexure VIII), as per the minutes received from the Secretary, Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat and forwards the same to the concerned Administrative Department of the Bill for approval. The Administrative Department will send it to the Secretary, Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat, for the approval of the Committee.
  • (6) After approval of amendments by the Committee, the amendments will be incorporated in the Bill (English and Marathi Bills) by concerned officers of the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.
  • (7) The officers of the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat will prepare the Report of the Joint Committee (in English and Marathi).

H 2316-9a Presentation of Report of Joint Committee.

  • 6.26 (1) The Report of the Joint Committee on a Bill will be presented to the House (in which motion for Joint Committee was passed) by the Minister-in-charge of the Bill. In presenting the Report, the Minister-in-charge may make brief explanatory remarks about the changes made in the Bill by the Joint Committee.
  • (2) The Minister-in-charge of the Bill will moves the motion for consideration of the Bill for the House.
  • (3) After a motion has been agreed to by the House, a Bill will be taken into consideration clause-by-clause.
  • (4) The Bill will be passed as per the regular procedure of passing of Bill by House (See procedure in 6.13 to 6.15).
  • (5) The Joint Committee Report of the Bill will be published in the Official Gazette, by the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat. Lapse / continuance of Bill 156/151 MLA/MLC Rules.
  • 6.27 (1) Before a Bill has been passed, if no motion in regard to it is made during the period of one year, the Bill will be lapsed.
  • (2) The concerned House may, on a motion by the concerned Minister, in the next following Session, make a special order for the continuance of the Bill. Seven clear days’ notice shall be necessary for such motion.
  • (3) A Bill in regard to which the House has made a special order shall be continued from the stage at which it was when lapsed. Procedure when Bill is circulated for eliciting opinion 125/121, MLA/MLC Rules.
  • 6.28 (1) Where a motion that a Bill be circulated for the purpose of eliciting opinion is carried and the Bill is circulated in accordance with that direction and opinions have been received thereon within the period specified, the Minister-in-charge if he wishes to proceed with the Bill thereafter shall move that the Bill be referred to a Select Committee unless the Speaker/Chairman, in exercise of his power to suspend this provision, allows him to make a motion that the Bill be taken into consideration.
  • (2) On a motion made under this rule, the general provisions of the Bill may be discussed in the light of the opinions received on the Bill.
  • (3) When the Bill is to be circulated for eliciting opinion, necessary action is taken by the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat and the Bill is processed as per the provisions of rule 125 of Assembly Rules/ rule 121 of Council Rules.

Power to correct patent errors.

  • 6.29 When a Bill is passed by the House, the Speaker under rule 138 of Assembly Rules, and the Chairman under rule 134 of the Council Rules, have power to correct patent errors and make such other changes in the Bill as are consequential on the amendment accepted by the House. Authentic copies of Bill 152/147, MLA/MLC Rules.
  • 6.30 When the Bill is passed by Both Houses or deemed to have been passed by Assembly and Council, the Authentic copies of the Bill will be signed by the Speaker/Chairman, as the case may be, in which House the Bill was introduced. The Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat will send Authentic copies of the Bill to the Secretary (Legislation) Law and Judiciary Department, for forwarding the same to the Governor for assent.

Governor’s assent to Bill.

  • 6.31 (1) The Secretary (Legislation) Law and Judiciary Department will send two Authentic copies of the Bill to the Governor along with letter addressed to the Secretary to the Governor for the assent to the Bill under Article 200 of the Constitution and one copy will be retained for the record in the file.
  • (2) A copy of the forwarding letter will be send to the concerned Originating Department for information.
  • (3) Along with the letter and Authentic copies, the following copies (Marathi and English) will be sent to the Governor,-
  • (a) One copy of Bill as introduced in the Maharashtra Legislative Assembly with Statement of Objects and Reasons.
  • (b) One copy of Bill as passed by the Maharashtra Legislative Assembly.
  • (c) Two copies of the Bill as passed by the Maharashtra Legislative Council.
  • (d) If the Bill was referred to the Select Committee /Joint Committee, the copy of the Report of Select Committee /the Joint Committee. President’s assent to Bill.
  • 6.32 (1) If the Bill requires to be reserved for the assent of the President under Article 254(2) of the Constitution, the Secretary (Legislation) Law and Judiciary Department will send three Authentic copies of the Bill to the President through Secretary to the Governor.
  • (2) Along with the letter and Authentic copies, the following copies (Marathi and English) will be sent to the Governor,––
  • (a) Ten copies of the Bill as introduced in the Maharashtra Legislative Assembly with Statement of Objects and Reasons.
  • (b) Ten copies of the Bill as passed by the Maharashtra Legislative Assembly.
  • (c) Ten copies of the Bill as passed by the Maharashtra Legislative Council.
  • (d) If the Bill was referred to the Select Committee /Joint Committee, the copy of the Report of Select Committee /the Joint Committee. Note.––Additional two copies of each stages of the Bill shall be sent along with the letter to Secretary to the Governor for office record and to take following.
  • (3) A copy of the forwarding letter will be sent to the concerned Originating Department for information and to the Special Commissioner to the Government of Maharashtra, Maharashtra Sadan, Copernicus Road, New Delhi, with two copies of the Bill, with a request to move the Government of India to obtain the assent of the President to the Bill and to convey the same to the Law and Judiciary Department.
  • 6.33 The Office of the Governor will send the Authentic copies of the Bill to the President for assent with forwarding letter. A copy of the forwarding letter will be sent to the Secretary (Legislation) Law and Judiciary Department and concerned Originating Department, for information.

DUTIES AND RESPONSIBILITIES OF ORIGINATING DEPARTMENT IN LEGISLATION AND SUBORDINATE LEGISLATION WORK

7.

  • (A) Duties and responsibilities of officers of Administrative Department in case of Bill.- The Originating Department shall have the following duties in the process of Legislative proposals,–

Intimate to Law and Judiciary Department.

  • (1) Intimate in advance to the Law and Judiciary Department about the proposed legislative proposals.

Formulation of Policy.

  • (2) Before preparing any legislative proposal formulate the policy first, containing purpose of the legislation, scope of the legislation, achievements, administrative approvals, financial aspects, etc.

Follow the time table.

  • (3) Follow the time frame specified in Chapter III while forwarding the legislative proposals to the Law and Judiciary Department. Consultation with other Department or Authorities.
  • (4) Take the concurrence of other Departments on administrative grounds, if necessary, before forwardings the file to the Law and Judiciary Department.

Approval of Finance Department.

  • (5) If any financial implications are involved in the proposal, take approval of the Finance Department.

Examine legal and constitutional feasibility of proposal.

  • (6) Before bringing any legislative proposal to the Cabinet, always refer the file to the Law and Judiciary Department, for examining the legal and constitutional feasibility of the proposal. Preparation of draft Bill with SOR.
  • (7) While forwarding the file to the Law and Judiciary Department always send along with file draft amendments with Statement of Objects and Reasons. Cabinet Note. (8) The Cabinet Note of every legislative proposal shall be forwarded to the Law and Judiciary Department (Legislation Branch). Forward draft to Law and Judiciary Department for scrutiny.
  • (9) (a) After Cabinet approval, prepare the draft Bill in English with Statement of Objects and Reasons, in duplicate, as per the Cabinet decision and forward the file to the Law and Judiciary Department (Legislation Branch) for scrutiny of the draft Bill along with the Cabinet decision.
  • (b) Forward the hard and soft copy of the draft (in duplicate) along with the file.
  • (c) Remain present for discussion while finalizing the draft in the Law and Judiciary Department (Legislation Branch).

Verification of draft.

  • (10) When the draft received from the Law and Judiciary Department, check draft carefully.

Communicate to Legislation Branch.

  • (11) If the draft Bill meets the requirements of the Department, communicate the same to the Law and Judiciary Department. If any changes/corrections are made in the draft, show it by ink.

Checking of copies.

  • (12) Check Marathi and English copies of the drafts forwarded by the Law and Judiciary Department.

Signature of Minister on Notice of Bill.

  • (13) Obtain the signature of the Secretary of the Department and concerned Minister on the Notice papers of the Bill and Governors recommendation.

Briefing to Minister.

  • (14) Brief to the concerned Minister in respect of the provisions of the Bill.

Citation of Bill.

  • (15) Collect the Citation Form from the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat and give to the Minister at the time of introduction and passing of Bill in the House.

Attending House while passing Bill.

  • (16) Remain present in the concerned House at the time of in introduction consideration/passing of Bill.

Checking of Gazette copies.

  • (17) Check Gazette copies of the Act after publication in the Official Gazette.

Bringing into force provisions of Act.

  • (18) If the Government has taken power in the Act to bring into force the provisions of the Act in future date, take necessary steps to issue notification, to bring into force the provisions of the Act with the approval of the concerned Minister.

Prepare Rules.

  • (19) If the Act provides to make rules, prepare draft rules within six months.
  • 7.2 (B) Duties and responsibilities of officers of Administrative Department in case of Ordinance. Formulation of Policy.
  • (1) Before preparing any legislative proposal to promulgate an Ordinance, formulate the policy first, it includes purpose of the legislation, scope of the legislation, achievements, administrative approvals, financial aspects, etc.

Provide urgency.

  • (2) Provide the urgency to promulgate an Ordinance in the file.

Prepare draft.

  • (3) Prepare the draft Ordinance with Statement.

Same duties provided for Bill.

  • (4) The same duties provided for Bill, above will be applicable for preparation and finalization of draft Ordinance.

President’s Instructions.

  • (5) If the provisions of Ordinance are repugnant to the Central Acts, obtain the Instructions of the President before promulgation of an Ordinance.

Submission of draft Ordinance to Governor.

  • (6) Submit draft Ordinance file to Governor, for signature.

Publication of Ordinance.

  • (7) After Governor Signature on the Ordinance, immediately handover the file to the Law and Judiciary Department for publication of an Ordinance alongwith Note.

Checking of Gazette Copies of Ordinance.

  • (8) Check Gazette copies of the Ordinance published in the Official Gazette.

Postpromulgation action.

  • (9) As soon as the Ordinance is promulgated, the following action shall be taken,-
  • (i) Shall take signature of the concerned Minister on the Motion supplied by the Law and Judiciary Department, to lay the Ordinance before both Houses of the State Legislature.
  • (iii) Shall send the said motion to the Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.

Conversion of Ordinance into Bill.

  • (10) In case, the Department has decided not to convert the Ordinance into Bill, communicate the same to the Law and Judiciary Department and the Parliamentary Affairs Department.
  • 7.3 (C) Duties and responsibilities of Administrative Department in case of Subordinate Legislation. Initiation of proposal.
  • (1) Initiate the proposal to issue notifications/rules/orders, etc., require under the Act. Use earlier similar precedents.
  • (2) While preparing the draft of notifications/rules/orders, etc., use/refer earlier similar precedents. Scrutiny/vetting of rules,
  • (3) Refer the files of notifications/rules/orders, etc., to the Law and Judiciary Department for its scrutiny/vetting. H 2316-10 notifications. Discussion with L&JD.
  • (4) To avoid delay in work, before sending file to the Law and Judiciary Department, if necessary, discuss the proposal with the concerned officer of the Legislation Branch.
  • (5) Remain present for discussion in the Law and Judiciary Department.
  • (6) Send two copies of draft alongwith file for scrutiny.

Laying of rules.

  • (7) Send the Gazette copies of the rules, notifications, etc., which are required to lay before both Houses of the State Legislature/Subordinate Legislation Committee to the Secretary, Maharashtra Legislature Secretariat.
  • (8) In case of recruitment rules, follow the procedure prescribed by the General Administration Department.

SALIENT FEATURES OF ACT

  • (A) Main parts of Act :–– The Principal Act generally contains the following parts. The officer who drafts the legislation shall know the parts of the Act and where to use the same. Long title. 8.1. Long title is an important part of the Act. Every Act has a long title, which indicates the subject matter of the Act and gives an important clue as to what the law is all about. The long title may be referred for the purpose of ascertaining its general scope and falling light on its construction. A comprehensive long title serves a valuable purpose to communicate the intended spirit and scope of the Act. The policy and purpose behind an enactment may be deduced from its long title. Long title gives an idea of what the Act proposes to deal with in a nutshell. It can be shorter or long depending upon the matters which should be briefly referred to explain the main purpose of the Act. It should be explanatory and give the background of the enactment. When a Bill is enacted long title becomes part of the Act. The long title can be used for the purpose of interpreting the provisions of the Act as a whole and ascertaining the meaning in the same manner as preamble is used. Since the long title is an indicator of the subject-matter of the Act, therefore care should be taken in drafting the long title. Preamble. 8.2. Preamble contains a detail background of the Act. The Preamble expresses the scope, object and purpose of the Act. It provides the ground cause of making law. The object of Preamble can be served equally well by the long title in almost all cases. The policy and purpose of an Act can be ascertained from the Preamble of the Act. It contains the ideas and aspiration of the legislation. Preamble can be used to interpret the provisions of the Act. Preamble is the part of the Act and can be read alongwith other provisions of the Act in case of interpretation of the provisions of the Act.

H 2316-10a Enacting formula.

  • 8.3. Enacting formula comes after long title and Preamble; it is always a very small portion of it. The pattern of enacting formula in State Act and Central Acts is different. In the State Acts, enacting formula is “it is hereby enacted in the Seventy –sixth Year of the Republic of India as follows:- In Central Acts, enacting formula is “BE it enacted by Parliament in the Seventy-sixth Year of the Republic of India as follows:—”. Short title. 8.4. Short title is the name of the Act. As a rule, every Act of the Legislature must have a short title, is usually ending with the year in which the Bill is introduced in the House. The purpose of the short title is to facilitate the reference and identification of the Act. It indicates the subject of the Act in very brief. Every Act is also indicated by the number assigned to it in the year in which it is published in the Official Gazette. Section 29 of the Maharashtra General Clauses Act, provides that in any Bombay Act or Maharashtra Act, and in any rule, by law, instrument or document made under, or with reference to, any Bombay Act or Maharashtra Act any enactment may be cited by reference to the title or short title (if any) conferred thereon or by reference to the number and year thereof. The drafting practice is to include the short title, the extent clause and the commencement clause, if any, in a single section divided into three sub-sections. Commencement clause.
  • 8.5. Commencement clause contains the provisions about the date of enforcement of the Act. In most of the cases, the date of the commencement of the Act is provided in the Act itself. In some cases, it is necessary to give retrospective effect to the provisions of the Act. In such cases, the retrospective date is provided in commencement clause of the Act. Section 5 of the Maharashtra General Clauses Act (I of 1904) contains provisions, when the provisions of the State Acts shall come into force.
  • (1) Act.- In certain cases, it may not be possible to bring into force all the provisions of the Act immediately. It is necessary to bring it into force on some convenient date in future. In such cases, the Government takes the power to appoint the date, by notification in the Official Gazette, when the Act shall come into force. Where the rules will have to be made; necessary functionaries will have to be appointed, provisions of Act shall have to bring into force step by step, sometimes the Act brings into force in different parts on different dates, and various other factors to be considered by the Government, in such cases, the Government takes power to issue notification in the Official Gazette, for bringing into force the provisions of the Act in future date. Different wordings are adopted in the commencement clause depending on the needs and circumstances of each case.
  • (2) Ordinance.–An Ordinance is promulgated as a emergency measure therefore, it has to come into force immediately. It is a customary practice to provide commencement clause in Ordinance as “It shall come into force at once”. It means the date on which the Ordinance is published in the Official Gazette, the provisions of the Ordinance shall come into force on that date. Extent. 8.6. Extent clause provides, where the Act shall be made applicable. It provides the jurisdiction of the Act. It indicates the area to which the Act shall be applicable. Maharashtra Acts, provides, “It extends to the whole of the State of Maharashtra”. Sometimes, the Act is made applicable for certain area of the State or excludes the certain area from application of Act. Amending Acts do not require extent clause as they would naturally have the same extent as the principal Acts which they amend. It is not necessary always to make the extent provision in Act. Duration. 8.7. Certain laws are enacted for certain duration or for short stipulated period. The duration of such Act is specified in this clause. Such laws cease to be effective after the expiry of the period stipulated therein. In exceptional cases this proviosn is made in Act. Application. 8.8. Application clause provides to whom the Act will be applicable. It provides the categories of persons, institutions, authorities, legal entities to whom the Act will be applicable. This provision is made as per necessity. Definitions. 8.9. Definitions clause usually comes after short title. Definitions are an important tool in legislative drafting. The purpose of the definition is to define the meaning of the words or expressions used in the Acts in order to avoid their repetitions or to confine specific meaning to such words or expressions or to exclude something from them. Definitions are intended to avoid ambiguities and by means of abbreviation, tedious repetitions. General definitions are defined in the General Clauses Act. (Maharashtra General Clauses Act See Annexture XXIII) If the definitions are defined in the General Clauses Act, it need not be defined again in the Act, if they have same meaning. If special meaning is needed of the term than the word defined in the General Clauses Act, then such terms are defined in the Act. The word defined in the definition clause will have the same meaning throughout the Act. The definition may be inclusive or exclusive. The meaning given to the word or phrase is applied wherever it occurs or appears in the Act, unless the contrary intention is clearly indicated in the context. More words should not be used than are necessary to make the meaning clear. Every superfluous word may raise a doubt and dispute in court. Different words should not be used to express the same thing. The same word should not be used with different meanings. Chapters/ Parts and Heading.
  • 8.10. When any Act is bulky and different subjects are covered in the Act, conveniently the provisions of the Act are divided into Chapters for sake of clarity. Sometimes headings are given to group of sections as per their contents in the Chapter. Marginal notes.
  • 8.11. Marginal note is the first guide for the user of the Act. Marginal notes are captions attached to every section. Marginal notes are convenient for the purposes of the reference. The object of the Marginal notes is to give clear indication of the contents of that section; it gets a fair idea of the contents of sections. It is a test to determine whether given subject should be dealt with one or more sections. Marginal note should be in short or brief and distinctive. It should not attempt to summarize the contents of the clause to which it relates. Marginal note should be brief, precise, distinctive, accurate and to the point.

Section, subsection, clause, etc.

  • 8.12. (a) Section–Section is the part of Act. Section is a substantive and an important part of an Act. Sections are drafted keeping in view the continuity of the subject dealt within an Act. While drafting a section, care must be taken that it should not be too long and if it is, then it is necessary to divide it further into sub-sections and clauses to bring clarity to the matter that is dealt with in the section. Section normally deals with a complete aspect of the subject such as the establishment of authority or constitution of statutory corporation or Board, its composition, its members and their powers and duties, qualifications, removal, etc. For the purpose of easy flow, understanding and clarity, the section is divided into sub-sections and sub-sections into clauses and sub-clauses and further into items as per requirement. However, the sub-division of section into parts and their nomenclature depends upon the choice of draftsman and there are no fixed rules in this respect.
  • (a) Sub-section.–Section is divided into sub-sections.
  • (b) Clause.–Sub-section is divided into clauses. Some time, section has no sub-sections but section is divided into clauses.
  • (c) Sub-clause.–Clause is divided into sub-clauses.
  • (d) Paragraph.–Sub-clause is divided into paragraph.
  • (e) Subparagraph.–Paragraph is divided into sub-paragraph. Article. 8.13. Articles are contained in the Constitution of India. Article is sub-divided in clauses and sub-clauses. Sometimes the Schedules are appended to the Acts; the entries in the Schedules are also called as Articles. e.g Schedule appended to Stamp Act, Entries in the Schedule are called Articles. Proviso. 8.14. A proviso is an exception to section or sub-section or clause. The purpose of the proviso is to qualify or make exception certain provisions of the main Act or section or sub-section or clause. A proviso is used to express that something which is not part of the section or sub-section or clause. Proviso is used to remove special cases from general provisions and to provide for them specially. The proviso should only be used to create an exception and not to create a condition. Proviso is used to qualify or make exception to certain provisions from the operation of main section or sub-section or clause. The proviso is used to limit the application of the enacted provision so as to except something which would have otherwise been within it or in some measure to modify the enacting clause or section. Sometimes a proviso may be imbedded in the main provision and becomes the integral part of it so as to amount to a substantive provision itself. The proviso should not travel beyond the provision to which it is made.

Explanation. 8.15. An Explanation is appended to section/sub-section/clause, to explain the meaning and intention of the main provision or words contained in the section/sub-section/clause. An Explanation is used where there is obscurity or vagueness in the main provision or to provide an additional support to the main provision and to make it meaningful and purposeful or to clarify the same as to make it consistent with the dominant object which it seems to subserve. An Explanation may be added to include something within or to exclude something from the ambit of the main provision or the connotation of some words occurring in it. It explains the meaning and intendment of the main provision. The purpose of an Explanation is to explain certain words, phrases or some aspect or concept of the main provision.

Illustrations. 8.16. Illustration to the section is part of the section and help to elucidate the principle of the section. It cannot have effect of modifying the language of section and they cannot either curtail or expand the ambit of section which alone forms the Act. Illustration also serves the same purpose as that of the Explanation. An illustration to a statutory provision merely illustrates a principle and cannot be exhaustive in terms of application of the provision. It illustrates the true scope and ambit of the section and has to be read subject to the relevant provision itself. Now a days Illustrations are used rarely in drafting of legislation.

Schedules. 8.17. The Schedules are appended at the end of the Act. The purpose of the Schedule is to avoid encumbering the sections in the Act with matters of excessive details. The Schedule is convenient device for including matters in detail which can’t be made in the main part of the Act. Schedules are attached to the Act to avoid unnecessary bulkiness. Schedules contain details and forms for working out policy underlying the sections of the Act.

  • (B) Other contents of Act Duration of Act.
  • 8.18 Duration clause provides the period for which the Act will remain in force. After that period the Act will expire. The general rule regarding duration of Act is that it continues in force until it is repealed. But where the Act is intended to be temporary, it will generally specify a date when it will expire or it may empower some authority to fix the date or it may provide for its expiry upon the occurrence of a contingency. Declaration. 8.19 Certain Act contains a declaratory clause, which declares the need or requirement for which the Act is enacted. Occasionally the Schedules contain such rules and forms which can be suitably amended according to local or changing by process simpler than normal one required for amending other parts of the Act. A Schedule should be given a description heading and should bear a reference to the sections or regulations which refer to it. Matters of principal Act should not be included in the Schedule. The forms to be prescribed, the rates at which tax/duties may be levied, the form in which oaths may be administered, the principles for determined compensation, amendments to be made in other Act where large number of amendments are involved, etc., are provided in the Schedules. The Schedule is normally read together with the provisions of the Act. Power to alter or modify or amend the Schedule is conferred in the Act subject to conditions. The Expression is used as “First Schedule” “Second Schedule” “Third Schedule” and not as “Schedule I”, “Schedule II”, “Schedule III”. Forms. 8.18. Forms are merely examples/pro forma. The forms provides the format for return to be submitted, applications under Act to be made, notice to be issued, etc. The format of Forms are appended to the Act at the end.

Annexure. 8.19. The Annexures are sometimes appended to the principal Act. It provides supplementary provisions read with the provisions of the Act.

H 2316-11 Establishment of Authority /Board.

  • 8.20. The Act provides for establishment of Authorities or constitution of Boards. In such cases, it is necessary to make provision of “composition of Authority/Board,” qualification of appointment, disqualification of members, term of office in case of non-official members, powers and duties, allowances to be paid, staff of Authority/Board, service conditions of employees, etc. Constitution/ establishment of legal entity.
  • 8.21. When any Corporation, Body or Authority is constituted in permanent nature or with perpetual succession is to be set up with power to hold and dispose of property, it is convenient to create legal entity of such Corporation, Body or Authority under the Act. In such cases, the following provision is made:- “the … Corporation/University/Authority shall be a body corporate having perpetual succession and a common seal, and may sue and be sued in its corporate name, and shall be competent to acquire, hold and dispose of property, both moveable and immoveable, and to contract and to do all things necessary for the purposes of this Act.”. Power to levy (fee, tax, etc.)
  • 8.22. (a) Tax–Article 265 of the Constitution of India provides that no tax shall be levied or collected except by authority of law. Every taxing statute has a charging section and procedure for collection of tax. There are three components of taxing statutes viz. subject of tax, person to pay the tax and the rate of tax is levied. In case of exemption, specific provision of exemption of tax should be made in Act. While drafting tax laws the provisions of Articles 246A, 285, 286, 287, 288 and 289 of the Constitution shall be considered. When any Act empowers the Government to levy tax, the rate of tax should be provided in section it-self or where there are slabs of tax, they may be provided in the Schedule of the Act. e.g. the Maharashtra Electricity Duty Act (Mah. XL of 1968), the Maharashtra Stamp Act (LX of 1958), tax is provided in Schedule. In case of exemption from payment of tax, specific provision should be made in the Act to give exemption from payment of tax.
  • (b) Fee.–When under any Act, fee is to be levied, specific power to levy of fee should be made in the Act. Entry 96 in List I, entry 66 in List II and entry 47 of List III of the Constitution empowers to levy fee. Fee may be levied for services rendered. There should be correlation for levy of fee and services rendered. General fee can be collected for making an application, filing of an appeal, etc.

Exemption from Act.

  • 8.23. The provision is made in the Act, to exempt/exclude certain establishments/persons or category of persons from the purview of the Act or from certain provisions of the Act. Sometimes it is necessary and desirable to vest the powers to the Government, to exempt from the operation of the Act any person, class of persons, property, institutions, goods, things, etc, as the application of the Act, for public purpose. However, it shall ensure that, it shall not violate Article 14 of the Constitution. Penal provisions.
  • 8.24. When regulatory laws are enacted, the provisions of offences for violation of the provisions of the Act are made by way of imposition of fine or penalty or punishment. Penalty is imposed, on violation of the provisions of the Act or rules, by the authorities to whom the power is conferred by the Act. It is applied both civil and criminal laws. Penalty refers as disciplinary measure for violation of provisions of Act. The penal provisions may be criminal or civil nature. When the provision of fine or imprisonment is made, the offences are tried by the courts. Sometime the provisions are made to impose penalty by the administrative authority or quasi-Judicial Authority for violation of the provisions of Act and rules which are civil in nature. In such cases, the provision of bar of jurisdiction by civil court is required to be made in the Act and the concerned authorities are empowered to impose penalty. e.g. Under the Real Estate (Regulation and Development) Act, 2016, the Real Estate Regulatory Authority is empowered to impose penalty for violation of the provisions of the Act. Offences by Company.
  • 8.25. A company is a legal entity. When in any Act, penalty is provided for violation of the provisions of the Act, in such cases, it is not necessary to distinguish between individual and a company. But when the punishment is provided as imprisonment, in such cases, to fix the responsibilities for the offences committed, a provision of offences by company is made. A person is being “in-charge of” and “responsible to” the company for the conduct of the business of the company is liable for the offence.

H 2316-11a Compounding of offences.

  • 8.26. Where penalty/fine is provided for violation of the provisions of the Act; the cases are filed in the court. If the intention of the Government is not to spent time in litigation, a provision of compounding of offences is made in the Act. The compounding of offences is not a matter of right. The offences are compoundable with or without the permission of the court, as provided in the Act. The offences which are not serious in nature, offences which are of technical nature caused by an act of omission, private nature offences, taxation related offences, revenue related offences, the intention of the legislature is not to keep the offender behind the bar, such offences are generally made compoundable. Power to issue directions.
  • 8.27. When the Government intends to give directions to the subordinate authorities for implement of the provisions of the Act, the provision of power to issue directions are made in the Act. This provision shall be made as per the necessity of the Government. Appeal. 8.28. An appeal is the process in which cases are decided by lower authority and are reviewed by a higher authority or by higher Court. An appeal is made from the decision of lower court to the higher Court or from subordinate authority/ officer, to the higher authority/officer. Unless enabling provision of appeal is made in the Act, no appeal can be filed from the orders of subordinate authority/ officer, to the higher authority/officer. The provisions of conditions for filing of an appeal, time limit for filing appeal, condolence of delay, etc, can be made in appeal section. Delegation of power/Subdelegation.
  • 8.29. Acts are enacted by State Legislature/Parliament. The State Legislature/Parliament delegates its powers to the Government or to the authorities subordinate to the Government. Delegation includes power to make rules, issue notifications, make bye-laws, issue orders, frame schemes, etc. The power conferred by the Act can’t be sub-delegated unless it is expressly provided in the Act.

Cognizance of offence.

  • 8.30. Cognizance of offence means taking notice, or becoming aware of the alleged commission of an offence. The court will have to take cognizance of the offence before it could proceed with the conduct of the trial. When the provision of an offence is made in any Act, for violation of the provisions of the Act, unless the Act specifically provides, such offences are tried as cognizable/non-cognizable, bailable/non-bailable, as per the provisions of First Schedule, II Classification of offences against other laws of the Bhartiya Nagrik Surkha Sanhita, 2023. The specific provision of offence, such as cognizable/noncognizable, bailable/non-bailable, can be made in the Act, as per the nature of the offence. The provision can be made in the Act, when the court can take the cognizance of the offence, like, “no Court shall take cognizance of an offence punishable under this Act except upon a complaint, in writing, made by a person authorised in this behalf by the Government”. Bar of jurisdiction.
  • 8.31. In cases where, Tribunals/Authorities are established or constituted to file complaint, appeals, imposition of penalty or fine which are civil in nature, the provisions of bar of jurisdiction of civil court is made in the Act. Where the Act gives finality to the orders of the tribunal or Authority, the civil court's jurisdiction are excluded from the Act by making provision of bar of jurisdiction. Overriding effect.
  • 8.32. When it is necessary to make different provision than the provisions of an existing Act, or when there would be inconsistency between the provisions of existing Act and the provisions of proposed law, in such cases, it is necessary to make provision in new law to give overriding effect over the provisions of existing Act. In cases, where it is not possible to examine all relevant provisions of Acts to decide which provision should be overruled and to what extent, the convenient drafting device is adopted whereby the particular Act is given overriding effect. Power to amend Schedule/ Forms.
  • 8.33. Sometime Schedules are appended to the Act. The Government intends to have flexibility to make changes in the Schedule or frequently changes are required to be made in Schedule, which are procedural in nature, in such cases, instead of going before Legislature for making such changes, the provision to amend Schedule is made in the Act. When the Schedule provides entries or forms or rate of tax, the power is taken with the State Government to amend the Schedule by notification published in the Official Gazette, it includes, to add any entry, to amend any entry or to delete any entry of the Schedule. The provision may also be made to lay down such notification before the Legislature.

Protection of action taken in good.

  • 8.34. Where Government servants or public servants honestly intends to act within his statutory powers, whether reasonably or not, believing in the existence of such facts, state of things as would, if really existing, have justified his conduct, special protection is provided in the Act. The provision of protection of action taken in good faith is made in the Act, so as to give protection from taking legal actions against them presuming that, the actions taken by them are in good faith or they are acting in accordance with the provisions of the law. Power to make rules/regulati ons/By-laws.
  • 8.35. Power to make rules, regulations, bye-laws is a delegation of powers by the State Legislature to the Government, Government Authorities, bodies, etc. The substantive/enabling provisions are made in the Act and the procedural part is made in the Rules, regulations, bye-laws. Such Rules, regulations, bye-laws are made by the Government, Authorities, bodies, etc. The provision is also made to lay down such rules before each House of the State Legislature. Repealed. 8.36. Repealing clause is a provision made in Act to repeal earlier law/statute. The spent Acts or outdated Acts are repealed by separate Act. Repealed may be express repeal, implied repeal or partial repeal. If the State Legislature is competent to enact the Act, it is competent to repeal the Act. Power to remove difficulties/ Removal of doubt.
  • 8.37. The power to remove difficulty section is a power delegated by the State Legislature to the Government to issue an Order in the Official Gazette, in cases where any difficulty arises while implementing the provisions of any new Act. The purpose of incorporating the said provision in the Act is, when any new law is enacted by the State Legislature, or when any law dealing with large schemes of social, industrial or other economic reforms, it may be difficult to foresee at the time of drafting all possible contingencies which may arise as a result of the new legislation, or the Legislature is not sure which difficulties the Government may face while implementing such law. If any difficulty arises while implementing the provisions of such law, the Government is empowered to issue an order by exercising the power under said section, so that the Act can be implemented smoothly. The Government can thereafter make appropriate amendments in the relevant sections by following the procedure, in due course. The Legislature has allowed the Government to exercise such power for limited period of time, generally two or three years from the commencement of the Act. It is permitted to clarify the doubts which may arise while implementing the provisions of the Act. By exercising the said power, the Government can slightly tinker with the Act to round off angularities, and smoothen the joints or remove minor obscurities to make the law workable, but it cannot change, disfigure the basic structure and primary features of the Act. In no case, under the guise of removing a difficulty, change the scheme and essential provisions of the Act. Every order issued under this section is required to lay before the House. Removal of doubts.
  • 8.38. This provision is made for excessive caution. While amendments are made in any Act, to remove the doubts which may arise while implementing the amended provisions of the Act, such provision is made in the Act. The object of making such provision is to meet unfounded objections and idle doubts. Transitional provisions.
  • 8.39. Where an Act contains substantive, amending or repealing Acts, it commonly also includes provisions which regulates the coming into operation of those enactments and modify their effect during the period of transition. A transitional provision modifies the operation of a new Act or other new legislation (or an amendment or repeal of an existing Act or other existing legislation) for a limited time. Transitional provisions deal with situations that exist before the new Act comes into force, sometimes by keeping the old rules in effect and sometimes by making new rules apply retroactively or immediately. The function of a transitional provision is to make special provision for the application of legislation to circumstances which exist at the time when the legislation comes into force. Basically, transitional provisions contain rules to ensure a proper and smooth transition from prior law to new law. The objective of such a provision is to bridge the gap between commencement of the Act and its operation prior to it. Transitional provision is incorporated in the Act when Act is enacted to provide how the Act will operate on facts and circumstances existing on the date it comes into operation or force. The operation of transitional provision is temporary; it becomes spent when all past circumstances with which it is designed to deal have been dealt. Savings. 8.40. Saving means to save all rights, the persons already have, not that gives him any new rights. Saving clause is made when any Act is repealed and certain rights, remedies or privileges already existing are saved. Saving provisions are a type of transitional provision, and they are generally used to retain an existing right or authority that might otherwise be affected by the new law. The saving provisions preserve the right or the authority despite the new law’s amendment or repeal of that former law. Saving clause is used to preserve from destruction of certain rights, remedies or privileges already existing.

Validation. 8.41. The validation clause is incorporated in the Act in situation where actions are carried out without lawful authority and in situation where they may be doubtful about lawfulness of their actions. By this clause, the actions taken by the authorities are validated. A competent Legislature can always validate a law which has been declared by Courts to be invalid, provided the infirmities and vitiating factors noticed in the declaratory Judgment are removed or cured. Such validating law can also be made with retrospective effect. Principles to be followed while making validating provisions:-

  • (1) A validating provision or Act may even make judgments and orders of courts ineffective provided it by retrospective effect removes the cause of invalidity or the basis which had led to those judgments.
  • (2) While drafting validating legislation, first step is to identify the nature and extent of actions or omissions, or both, which have produced the illegality that is to be cured.
  • (3) The second step is to identify the extent of the illegality caused by those actions or omissions.
  • (4) The validating legislation should do no more than remedy the defect or irregularity which is sought to be remedied.
  • (5) Validating legislation must be by its nature necessarily have retrospective effect.
  • (6) While drafting legislations having retrospective effect, regard must be had to actions and omissions which have taken place before and circumstances existing at the time when the legislation comes into force.
  • (7) Care must be taken to the need for indemnity provisions which should be included where it is desired to free from liability any person who has exposed himself to civil or criminal proceeding. Ordinance repealed clause.
  • 8.42. Due to temporary nature of Ordinance, it needs to be converted into Act by introducing an Ordinance conversion Bill for permanent effect. In Ordinance conversion Bill, it is necessary to repeal the Ordinance which is in force. The Ordinance repealing clause is added with saving provisions as the last clause in the Bill. In saving provision, the actions taken or things done under the Ordinance are saved. _______________________________________________________________ Note:- Some contents of this Chapter are taken from the Books of Legislative DraftingB.E. Atre, Legislative Drafting-Shaping the Law for the New Millennium-T.K.Viswanathan, Principles of Legislation and Legislative draftingN.K.Chakrabarti, Interpretation of StatutesJ. P. Singh, Legislative DraftingG.C.Thornton.

LEGAL PHRASES, GRAMATICAL WORDS USED IN DRAFTING

The following legal phrases and terms are frequently used in Acts. The purpose and meaning of the legal phrases and terms are explained below. While drafting of any legislation, it shall be considered by the draftsman. Mutatis mutandis (Referential legislation).

  • 9.1. Special Acts are enacted for specific purposes. Like the Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act, 2013 (LARR Act) is enacted for land acquisition and payment of compensation. The detail procedure of land acquisition is provided in the said Act. Certain provisions of the Maharashtra Land Revenue Code, 1966 provides for recovery of land revenues. Certain State Acts provide the provisions for land acquisition or recovery of revenues or taxes of Government. In such cases, instead of repeating the same provisions of the LAAR Act or the Land Revenue Code it can be provided that, the provisions of the LAAR Act shall be applicable mutatis mutandis for land acquisition or the provisions of the Land Revenue Code shall be applicable mutatis mutandis for recovery of taxes or Government dues. As he deems fit; thinks necessary, consider necessary.
  • 9.2. “As he/it deems fit” phrase is very often used. Such phrase denotes that the concerned person or authority has discretionary power in deciding a particular case or giving its or his decision in the matter. The words ‘as it deems fit’, ‘think necessary’ and ‘consider necessary’ have been, time and again used to confer discretion on the concerned authority. Having regard to.
  • 9.3. The phrase “having regard to” is used while giving some discretionary power to an executive authority or the court instead of specifying the conditions which are to be taken into consideration by the executive authority before using the power conferred on or the decision to be given by the court. The words ‘having regard to’ when occurring in the Act should be construed in relation to the context and the subject matter. Subject to a contrary context.
  • 9.4. The normal practice is a drafting to provide for unless there is anything repugnant while defining any other terms used in the Bill. When a word has been defined in the definition clause prima facie, that definition governs whenever that word is used in the body of the statute. When a particular term in the Act is to define (whether by enlarging or by restricting the ordinary meaning of a word or expression) then that term must, in the absence of clear H 2316-12 indication to the contrary be given the meaning intended. A defined word when used in the body of the Act may have to be given a meaning different from that contained in the definition clause, in order to cover such eventuality, a qualification is added to the definition clause that the word defined therein will have the meaning as per the definition unless there is anything repugnant in the subject or context, or unless the context otherwise requires. As soon as possible.
  • 9.5. The phrase “as soon as possible” refers to the time frame. To do a thing “as soon as possible” means to do it within a reasonable time, with an undertaking to do it in the shortest practicable time. It is always advisable to use “within reasonable time” instead of “as soon as possible”. Except where otherwise expressly enacted.
  • 9.6. The phrase “Except where otherwise expressly enacted” is used to give an effect to the express provision found anywhere in the Act or in any other law. It is an attempt that the provisions of the Act will be subjected to any specific provision in other laws. Without prejudice to the generality of the preceding.
  • 9.7. A provision enacted, ‘without prejudice to another’ has not the effect of affecting the operation of the other provision and any action taken under it must not be inconsistent with such other provision. The effect of such a clause is not to cut down the preceding general power. Save as otherwise expressly provided.
  • 9.8. The phrase “save as otherwise expressly provided” is often used in Acts to indicate that a certain provision applies, unless there is another provision that specifically overrides or modifies it. It essentially means that the provision in question is the default, unless there is something else that says otherwise. “any”, “all”, “each” and “every”.
  • 9.9. “Any”, “all”, “each” and “every” words are frequently used unnecessarily, for example, do not use “any” where “a” or “an” would serve as well. “Any” may properly be used where it is desired to emphasize that provision is of universal application or without qualification. “and”, “or” 9.10. As a general rule “and” or “or” should only appear after the penultimate of a series of paragraphs. “and” means all of a list of items. “or” means any one or more of a list of items. Do not use “and/or”; instead use “or” “before”, “after”, “from”
  • 9.11. The terms “before” and “after” are construed so as to exclude the day specified.

The term “after” is to be preferred instead of “from” because “from” is equivocal. The term ‘from” is used to refer to the place where someone or something starts or originates or show distance or separation in space. “deem” 9.12. The term “deem” may be used to create a legal fiction by deeming something to be that which it is not or deeming something not to be that which it is, but should not be used as an alternative to “judge”, “conclude”, “consider”, “believe” or “count” or in a definition to take the place of “include”; The term “deem” create artificiality. Instead of “deem” use “thinks” or “consider” or “construed”. “except” and “unless”

  • 9.13. The words “except” and “unless” are used if provision is made which is effective unless something happens or except in specific circumstances, it must consider whether it is necessary to provide what is to happen if that something does happen or those circumstances do occur. “existing”, “before” and “after”
  • 9.14. The terms “existing”, “before” and “after” should be related to a particular time or the happening of a particular event. The term “existing” should be used to particular point of time. “if” 9.15. The term “if” is very generally used to introduce a condition in ordinary everyday use. “immediately” 9.16. The term “Immediately” means with all reasonable speed considering the circumstances of the case. “less than” 9.17. Instead of “less than” use “not exceeding” or “exceeding”. “means” and “includes”
  • 9.18. Means.- The term “Means” is exclusive. The word “Means” is appropriate where the stipulated meaning is expressed in a complete form and no part of the intended meaning is omitted. When a word is defined to ‘mean’ such, the definition is prima facie restrictive and extensive. The use of the word ‘means’ shows that no other meaning can be assigned to the expression than is put in the definition. The draftsman must use the word ‘means’ where he wants to exhaust the significance of the term defined. Include.–‘Include’ is used in order to enlarge the meaning of the words or phrases occurring in the body of the Act. When it is so used these words or phrases must be construed as comprehending, not only such things as they signify according to their nature and import, but also those things, which the definition clause declares that they should include. H 2316-12a When the word ‘include’ is used in definition clause, the term defined retain its ordinary meaning and its scope is widen by specific enumeration of certain matters which its ordinary meaning may or may not comprise so as to make the definition enumerative but not exhaustive. Term defined with “include” will retain its ordinary meaning but its scope would be extended to bring within it matters, which in its ordinary meaning may or may not comprise. In definition clause the terms “means” and “includes” combined should not be used. “notwithstanding”, 9.19. The term “notwithstanding”, is used where one provision is to prevail over another or while giving overriding effect to the provisions over other provisions. It is also called the non obstante clause. The object of term “notwithstanding” is to give it overriding effect over other provisions of the Act which is equivalent to saying inspite of those provisions the particular clause would have a full operation. The expression “notwithstanding anything contained in the Act” is contradiction to the phrase “Subject to the provisions of this Act”. “subject to” 9.20. The term “subject to” is used when the sub servant provision is used. These expressions should be used to indicate which of the provisions is to prevail. “Subject to” conveys that the provision would yield to another provision. “without prejudice to”
  • 9.21. The phrase “without prejudice to” is used when no priority needs to be given over another provision. “on or after” “on or before”
  • 9.22. The terms “on” or “after” should be used when it is desired to include the specified. The terms “on or before” used to fix a certain period at one end. “oral” “verbal” 9.23. The term “oral” is used in relation to mouth and “verbal” is used in relation to word. “practical” “practicable”
  • 9.24. “practical” means capable of being accomplished.

“practicable” means capable of being accomplished under given circumstances. The words shall be used accordingly. “same” 9.25. The term “same” or “the same” should never be used as substantiate for the pronouns “it” or “them”. “such” 9.26. The term “such” is used adjectively to refer back to a noun already used.

Do not use “such” if “the” “a” or “that” will do. Do not use “such” to refer to a noun used in another sentence. Do not use “such” as substitute for “the” or “that”. “shall” and “may”

  • 9.27. The term “shall” to be used for mandatory provisions. The word “shall” should be used only when obligation is to be imposed on a person. A duty, mandate, obligation, requirement or condition precedent is expressed “shall”. Use “shall” if the verb it qualifies is in the active voice. Use “shall not” to express a prohibition. The term “may” be used for permissive provisions. The term “may” should be used when a permission, benefit, right, or privilege is given. The term “may” is used to confer a power, privilege, or right. “that” 9.28. The term “that” is used to introduce a restrictive clause modifying the nears antecedent. “which” 9.29. The term “which” is used to introduce a non restrictive clause. “when” 9.30. The term “when” is used when a single or rare occurrence of the event is contemplated. “where” 9.31. The term “where” is commonly used to introduce the cases or circumstances in which law is to apply. It is used in cases where frequent occurrences of the event are contemplated. “while” 9.32. The term “while” is used substitute “although” or “and”. “within” 9.33. The term “within” may be followed by “of” “after” or “from”.

Words not to use

9.34.

Do not use Use

Absolutely null and void and of no effect Void

Accorded Given Adequate number of Enough Adjudged, ordered, and decreed Adjudged Admit of Allow

Among and between Among (if more than two involved); between (if two or more but treated individually) Approximately About At the place where Where By means of By Cause it to be done Have it done Constitute and appoint Appoint Do and perform Do Does not operate to Does not During such time as While During the course of During Endeavor (as a verb) Try enter into a contract with contract with evidence, documentary and otherwise evidence

evince Show Except that But For the reason that Because Forthwith Immediately Frequently Often Full and adequate (or, full and complete) Full

Herein In this Act (or section or item)

Words to be avoided

  • 9.35. Words to be avoided The following legal words should be avoided in drafting -
  • (a) “aforesaid”, “said”, “abovementioned” and “aforementioned”;
  • (b) “herein”, “hereinafter”, “hereinbefore” and “hereby”; and
  • (c) “where to fore”, “where so ever”, “howsoever”, “whosoever”, “whoever” and “whatsoever”.
  • (d) Do not use qualifiers, such as “will”, “should” “must” and “ought”.
  • (e) Avoid “the provisions of” Instead of the words “in accordance with the provisions of this section,” say “under this section.”.

Note.–The some contents of this Chapter are taken from the Books of Legislative DraftingB.E. Atre, Legislative Drafting-Shaping the Law for the New Millennium-T.K.Viswanathan, Principles of Legislation and Legislative draftingN.K.Chakrabarti and from websites.

NUMBER, DATES AND PUNCTUATION

The basic rule of drafting is that a statute is always speaking. Use the present tense in drafting. Sentence. 10.1. The sentence should be placed in the following order- (i)case (ii) condition (iii) legal subject (iv) legal action. Spelling. 10.2. Spelling should be standardized as far as possible British English should be used. In other cases of permissible alternative spelling, use the first version adopted in the Shorter Oxford Dictionary. Latin and other foreign languages should be avoided but, if absolutely necessary, should be in italics. Capitals. 10.3. The use of capital in long title, headings and marginal notes.

  • (1) The words “Bill”, “Act” “Law”, “Part”, “Proclamation”, “Order”, “Schedule” and “Table” always begin with capitals.
  • (2) The words “Regulations”, “Rules”, “Bye-laws”, beginning with capitals when referring to a collection of provisions, but not when referring to particular provision. Numbers. 10.4. (1) Use numeral rather than words for numbers in the text of Bills, Rules, Regulations, Notices, Orders, etc. This includes dates, times, amounts, percentages and citations to the chapter and section numbers of statutes.
  • (2) References to specific dates should always be in the following form:- “1st January 2021”. For example :–“This Act shall come into force on the 1st July 2022.”
  • (3) Use numerals even for numbers from 1 to 10 (this is for consistency and is the practice followed). The only exceptions, (like “Seventy-first Year” and “Seventy-second Year”). Brackets ( ) 10.5. Sub-sections, clauses, sub-clauses should be written in brackets. In Amendment Act, Amendment number shall be written in brackets. Apostrophe ‘s’
  • 10.6. Apostrophe is placed before the ‘s’, to show possession;

example Governor’s Office.

Full stops (.) 10.7. (1) Full stops (.) This ends the sentence. It also used at the end of abbreviated word, example “Dr.” “per cent.” “i.e.”

  • (2) At the end of the marginal notes full stop should be given.
  • (3) Headings, including headings of Parts, should not be followed by a full stop. Coma (,) 10.8. (1) The coma (,) must serve a clearly identifiable and necessary purpose.
  • (2) Commas is use to separate one group of words from another. The proper use of coma gives the sentence clarity.
  • (3) The use of commas is particularly important since this form of punctuation can often be used to remove an ambiguity; commas serve two distinct purposes, namely, to separate and to enclose.
  • (4) Ordinarily, do not use a comma before “and” or “or” to separate the last of a conjunctive series of three or more words, phrases or clauses in a sentence. Example “men, women and children.”. Semicolumn (;)
