  • 10.9. (1) Semi-column (;) is used to separate related sentences, it is inserted instead of using a conjunctive word like “and”.
  • (2) Semicolons can also replace commas when listing items that already use commas, such as listing cities and states.
  • (3) Semicolons can be used to divide the items of a list if the items are long or contain internal punctuation. The semicolon helps readers keep track of the divisions between the items.
  • (4) Use a semicolon between closely related independent clauses which are not joined by a coordinating conjunction. Dash ( - ) 10.10. Use a dash (-) (not colon) to introduce a list of items. Dash should be the shorter not hyphen (-), not the longer version. It is also important to distinguish between dashes and hyphens. Hyphens are shorter lines (-); they are most often used to show connections between words that are working as a unit (for example, you might see adjectives like “well-intentioned”) or to spell certain words (like “e-mail”). Quotation (“”)
  • 10.11. Use quotation marks when defining a word or phrase.

Column (:) 10.12. Column (:) is used to introduce a list.

Division of Section, Rule, Regulation, Notification.

  • 10.13. Division of Section, Rule, Regulation, Notification.- The contents of the Act, Schedule, Rules, Regulations and Notification or Orders are divided in the following manner :-

No. Acts Schedule Rules Regulations Notification,

Orders and other Instruments

1 Section Entry Rule Regulation Paragraph

2 Sub-section Sub-entry Sub-rule Sub-regulation Sub-paragraph

3 Clause Clause Clause Clause Clause

4 Sub-clause Sub-clause Sub-clause Sub-clause Sub-clause

5 Paragraph Paragraph Paragraph Paragraph Item

6 Subparagraph Subparagraph Subparagraph Sub-paragraph Sub-item

7 Item Item Item Item

8 Sub-item Sub-item Sub-item Sub-item

H 2316-13

GUIDELINES FOR DRAFTING OF LEGISLATION

Drafting. 11.1 The drafting of legislation requires not only in-depth study of the subject-matter of the legislation but also fairly good idea about the laws in existence. The law to be understood by the common man therefore it must be precise and concise and must use simple language. There are number of rules which the draftsman should remember while drafting legislation to safeguard against committing drafting errors. The draftsman shall consider the following rules while drafting of any legislation. Preparation before drafting.

  • 11.2 (A) Preparation before drafting:- The Draftsman shall,-
  • (1) Get a clear concept of the legislative proposal.
  • (2) Identify and study the factual issues on the subject-matter of the legislative proposal.
  • (3) Study legal limitations that may apply to enact State laws.
  • (4) Prepare conceptual outline including all major ideas and objectives of the legislation and outline the remedies sought to be achieved by proposed legislation.
  • (5) Identify the person to whom the law will be applicable.
  • (6) Check current laws of other States on the subject matter of legislation.
  • (7) Discuss issues of legislation with the concerned officers of Administrative Department, subordinate authorities or implementing authorities.
  • (8) Check current Acts which would be affected by the proposed legislation.
  • (9) Examine all aspects of expediency specified in para 4.2 of Chapter IV of this Manual.
  • (10) Note down all the relevant points. Drafting of legislation.
  • 11.3 (B) Drafting of legislation:- At the time of drafting,-
  • (1) Examine whether similar provisions have been made in the existing State Acts or Central Acts.

H 2316-13a

  • (2) If amendments to be made in the Principal Act, before preparing the draft amendments, study the provisions of Act/relevant provisions, understand the scheme of the Act.
  • (3) In case of amending legislation decide the placement of amendments.
  • (4) In case of Principal Legislation, prepare the framework of law;

arrange sections/clauses as per subject matter (Prepare Index of sections).

  • (5) Refer the settled interpretation words and expressions used in existing laws and follow them to minimise the litigation and improve the quality of drafting.
  • (6) Take into consideration the legal principles, judicial decisions of the High Courts and Supreme Court on the subject matter of the amendments or proposed legislation, so that ambiguities can be removed while drafting.
  • (7) Discuss the provisions of draft with the officers of Administrative Department and subordinate officers who would implement the law.
  • (8) Examine whether the proposed provisions are consistent with the existing law, and whether any provision of proposed law are repugnant to the existing laws or earlier laws made by the Parliament, etc.
  • (9) Take into consideration what will be the consequences or effect of amendments or empact of law.
  • (10) Identify the terms that would require to be defined.
  • (11) Check the terms already defined in other Acts or in case of amendments in the same Act.
  • (12) Draft the text of the proposal in clear language after preparing conceptual outline.
  • (13) Divide amendments into sections/sub-sections/clauses/subclauses /paras/ sub-paras and items as per requirement.
  • (14) Provide Marginal Notes to each section.
  • (15) Provide citation, where reference of other Act is made in draft.

For State Act provide citation in Roman Numerals and for Central Acts in Arabic numerals. (e.g. Mah. XXI of 2025, 21 of 2025).

  • (16) When amendments are giving retrospective effect, then to verify the amendments should not give effect earlier than the date of commencement of the Principal Act or the date on which the relevant section (in which amendment is proposed) came into force.
  • (17) Use style and language compatible to existing legislation, avoid archaic expressions.
  • (18) Divide the subject into parts to achieve clarity.
  • (19) In case of amending legislation, verify whether the consequential amendments are required to be made in the Principal Act or in any other Acts.
  • (20) After finalisation of draft, give draft to the colleague/ third person/independent person, who knows the drafting, for examination.
  • (21) It shall also verify,–
  • (a) Whether it is necessary to incorporate commencement clause in Bill.
  • (b) Whether it is necessary to provide definitions of the terms used in the draft.
  • (c) Whether to make provision of removal of doubt clause.
  • (d) Whether to make provision of power to remove difficulties.
  • (e) Whether it is necessary to make provision of saving clause.
  • (f) In case of repeal of any Act, whether repealing clause is incorporated and whether saving clause is required.
  • (g) Whether transitional provisions are required to be made.
  • (h) Whether to incorporate rule making power. General rules for legislative drafting.
  • 11.4 (C) General rules for legislative drafting:- The work of a draftsman is akin to that of an architect. To achieve simplicity in language, it shall follow the following rules to make the legislation simple, concise and meaningful :–
  • (1) Before actually drafting a Bill, it is always advisable to prepare a rough “scheme” of the Bill. If the Bill is to be divided into Chapters, the heading of each Chapter and the subject matter of each of the clauses which is to be included into each Chapter should be noted. At this stage, it is not necessary to draft the clauses. It is necessary to note down the subject-matter of each clause. Care should be taken that the clauses are in a logical sequence, and follow the usual pattern of Bill.
  • (2) The Bill should be drafted in clear, simple and precise language.

It must use correct language/expressions. Clarity determines ease of understanding and removes ambiguity.

  • (3) Drafting must have accuracy and precision.
  • (4) The sentence should be simple. Each section should preferably contain a single provision. Long sentences should be avoided. The sentence may be split into sub-division that follow the progression of the reasoning, since a compact block of text is hard for the eye to follow and the mind to absorb.
  • (5) Make the structure of the sentence in such a way that the meaning is not made dependent on internal punctuation. Punctuation is generally a source of ambiguity.
  • (6) The terminology used in particular law should be consistent internally and with other laws, especially in the same field.
  • (7) Identical concept should be expressed in the same terms as far as possible without departing from the meaning in the ordinary, legal or technical languages. Formal consistence requires the same term to be used for the same concept. Substantial consistence requires that the Act does not contain inherent contradiction between its provisions.
  • (8) Complex words or terms should not be used, where one or more simple words convey the same meaning.
  • (9) The words used in the legislation should be unambiguous, clear and explicit to avoid taking help of rules of interpretation. The provision of a section should be such worded that its meaning is immediately understood by the person affected, the Courts and the implementing authorities.
  • (10) Section is a basic part of the Act. It communicates a simple or even a complex idea/ circumstance/ situation to the readers in a few sentences. The language of the section must be brief.
  • (11) Each section should contain one idea only. If an idea is complicated, it should be expressed in a series of sub-sections. A sub-section should not normally contain more than one sentence.
  • (12) Each sentence should be short and as simple as possible.

Language of section should be precise but not too technical.

  • (13) Sections and sub-sections must be as short as the clarity and quality of the statements will allow them.
  • (14) Sections may be arranged under a heading to the extent that they deal with the same subject-matter which makes the Act easier to understand.
  • (15) The sections of the Act should be arranged with precise clarity which will resolve the possible conflicts between various provisions of the Act. Provisions which diatonically oppose each other must never be used.
  • (16) The marginal note of a section should be in short and descriptive of its contents. It must be precise and accurate. It does not summarize the section but merely indicated the key words. The marginal note of a section normally should not contain any verb.
  • (17) The sections should be numbered consecutively in Arabic numerals.
  • (18) A long and complex section should be cut into sub-sections.

The sections are to be divided into sub-sections and paragraphs. Sub-sections are numbered with Arabic numerals in brackets preceded by the number of the section of which they are part.

  • (19) Paragraphs are indicated by Roman letters placed in brackets.
  • (20) Use express provisions in the Statute carefully and only when it is necessary and conducive to the object of the Act, because an express provision in the Act which includes the operation of certain provisions cannot be made nugatory by referring to general law.
  • (21) The Act should be concise and contents provisions should be as uniform as possible. The language of the Act should be consistent.
  • (22) A basic law should not contain detail provision. The detail provisions should be made by subordinate legislation i.e. by rules, regulation or by-laws. The decision to include detail provisions in an Act should be decided in the particular context.
  • (23) The Act should be consistent with other legislations and should not overlap or conflict with other laws which are applicable.
  • (24) Defined terms must be used in a uniform manner. Definitions only apply to the particular Act. The definitions used in the Act should not be out of the context.
  • (25) The provisions of Act should be in gender-neutral language. A law speaks of both women and men. The law should avoid any identification of a subject with any gender unless it is required in the peculiar circumstances described therein. If the substance of legislative sentence applies only to one gender; then the words that identify that gender should be used. Clearly, some provisions will apply to only men while some only to women.
  • (26) The punctuation should be sober and not excessive. Punctuation is an element of the sentence. It must not include expressive signs such as exclamation marks. An Act is neutral and its punctuation should be simple, justified and uniform. If the meaning of a provision can be changed simply by changing the punctuation, the provision must be re-written.
  • (27) Parentheses or brackets shall not be used as punctuation marks because they interrupt the flow of the sentence. Parentheses are normally used to add non-essential items or information.
  • (28) It is preferable for an Act to speak in the active voice.

Moreover, this is essential when a duty is imposed or a power is conferred. However, use of passive voice should not automatically be rejected. It is perfectly justified in case of general prohibitions or where the context clearly designates the person responsible for applying the rule.

  • (29) An Act speaks in the present tense in drafting laws. Use preset tense. Present tens are often clearest and most certain in vesting powers and imposing obligations. The future and the past tense are used only to express the temporal relationship between two events.
  • (30) An Act does not express desires, wishes or justification. It orders, authorizes, prohibits, governs or imposes penalties.
  • (31) Words take on the character of those on whose company they are to be found. Therefore, words should be used very carefully. Different words should not be used to express the same idea. The same word should not be used to express different ideas.
  • (32) Only acronyms that are commonly used and familiar may be used in the Act. The inclusion of acronyms in the Act often looks cosmetic, but reduces the capacity to convey the intention of the legislature clearly.
  • (33) References should be avoided whenever possible and as a rule no references should be made to the ‘preceding’ or the ‘following’ section, unless absolutely necessary.
  • (34) References to a provision are made by giving a complete description of it without adding ‘of this Act’ or ‘of this section’, unless such an expression is necessary to ensure that no confusion arises from a reference to another Act.
  • (35) Courts, generally give natural meaning or ordinary meaning to the words of the Act. The courts generally do not like to add or treat any word as obtuse. Therefore, care should be taken that the words of the Act carry plain and natural meaning.
  • (36) The stages of a procedure must be presented in their chronological order.
  • (37) The movement should be from the general to the particular, it must also be seen that the movement should be from the law to the procedure, from the rule to the exception, from the permanent to the temporary, from the abstract to the concrete, from the normal to the exceptional. In each case, the movement should be flexible and orderly without any vacuum or gap.
  • (38) The Act will first state the rule and then indicate the authority responsible for applying it and the procedure to be observed in applying it followed by the penalty for non-compliance.
  • (39) Transitional provisions deal with situations that exist before the new Act comes into force, sometimes by keeping the old rules in effect and sometimes by making the new rules apply retroactively or immediately.
  • (40) A Bill should not be divided into parts unless the subjects of the parts are so different that they might appropriately be embodied in separate Act. A division of an Act into parts may affect its construction by indicating the scheme of arrangement.

H 2316-14

  • (41) Codes and major Acts are divided into Chapters. Division of Act into Chapters helps in understanding the provisions of the Act. When any Act is bulky and different subjects are covered, conveniently the provisions of the Act may be divided into Chapters for sake of clarity.
  • (42) Each part should be separated from the preceding one by a space between the lines. It should be noted that it is important to choose an arrangement and that be uniform and systematically applied.
  • (43) Use non-obstante clause very carefully, it may cause difficulties as to which enacting paragraph is to be given overriding effect.
  • (44) Avoid legislation by reference. The object of drafting is not to save trouble but to make meaning clear. Difficulties may arise where provisions of one enactment are incorporated in another by referential method and incorporated enactment undergoes amendment subsequently.
  • (45) The long title should be provided to each Bill. The long title is an indicator of the subject matter of the legislation; it gives clue as to what the statute is all about, therefore, great care should be taken in drafting of long title.
  • (46) The Preamble should be provided to Bill. The Preamble expresses the scope, object and purpose of the Act. It provides the ground and cause of making law; therefore, care should be taken while drafting Preamble.
  • (47) Short title should be provided to each Bill. Short title is a name of the Act. The short title of the Act is to facilitate the reference and identification of the Act. Every Act of Legislature has a short title ending with the year in which it is enacted.
  • (48) The commencement clause should be drafted according to the need of application of the provisions of the Act. If Act is required to be brought into force immediately i.e. on the date of publication of the Act in the Official Gazette, then it is not necessary to provide commencement clause in the draft. If the intention is to bring the provisions of Act on future date then, the commencement clause should be provided. In that case, the power should be taken to issue notification in the Official Gazette, to specify the date.
  • (49) An Ordinance is promulgated as an emergency measure therefore; it has to come into force immediately. In Ordinance, commencement clause provides, it shall come into force at once.
  • (50) The proviso should only use to create an exception and not to create a condition. Proviso is used to confine or taking special cases out of general provision. Proviso is used to qualify or make exception to certain provisions from the operation of main section or sub-section. The proviso should not travel beyond the provision to which it is made. It uses to qualify or except certain provisions from the operation of the main section.
  • (51) An Explanation should be appended to section to explain the meaning and intention of the main provision or words contained in the section. When there is obscurity or vagueness in the main part or to provide an additional support to the main provision and to make it meaningful and purposeful, Explanation may be added. Explanation may be added to include something within or to exclude something from the ambit of the main provision or the connotation of some words occurring in it.
  • (52) Schedules contain lists of items that are not included in the body of the Act. Schedules are numbered consecutively in Roman numerals and the heading of the Schedule is followed by an indication in italics of the provision of the Act to which they refer. The Schedules are appended at the end of the Act. The Schedule can be amended by issuing a notification in the Official Gazette, if such power is confirmed in section of the Act.
  • (53) The Statement of Objects and Reasons is appended to each Bill.

Statement of Objects and Reasons briefly explains the purpose of the proposed legislation in short. The Statement is explanatory of the contents and objectives of a Bill and helps in understanding the necessity and scope of the Bill. It gives an idea or background of the provisions of the Bill or amendments. It is, therefore, required to be drafted in a non-technical language.

H 2316-14a

  • (54) The Memorandum Regarding Delegated Legislation shall be appended to the Bill, providing the details of the powers delegated by the Legislature to the Government or to the subordinate Authorities.
  • (55) The Financial Memorandum shall be appended to the Bill, where financial implications are involved. It provides the recurring or non-recurring expenditure which would involve after enactment of Bill.
  • (56) The general guidelines for drafting are as follows,-
  • (a) Use ordinary/simple language; avoid technical language, i.e. every word may be used wherever possible.
  • (b) Don’t use more words than necessary to make the meaning clear. Ever superfluous word may raise discretion /interpretation in courts.
  • (c) Nouns may be used in preference to pronouns.
  • (d) Don’t use same word with different meanings.
  • (e) Use singular words.
  • (f) Use common words.
  • (g) Avoid complicated words.
  • (h) Use “shall” to impose duty. Confine to use word “shall” to be imperative.
  • (i) Use “may” to grant discretion, authority to act.
  • (j) Use positive rather than negative.
  • (k) Avoid compound construction.
  • (l) Use short sentences and simple words to the extent possible.
  • (m) Use proviso sparingly. Provisos are great source of doubt and create ambiguity. Proviso upon proviso would make confusion.
  • (n) Choose terms absolute and not relative so as to leave the minimum opportunity for difference of opinions.
  • (o) Don’t give detail reasons for law, these opens the doors to controversy or interpretation.
  • (p) The standard formats of amendments are given in Annexure I, shall be followed while making amendments in any Act. Guidelines for drafting of Amending Legislation.
  • 11.5 (D) Guidelines for drafting of Amending Legislation :–While making amendments in any Act, the following rules shall be followed :– Long title of Bill.
  • 11.5.1 The long title of the Bill depends on the subject matter of the Bill and contents of the Bill, etc. Detail information about long title is explained in para 8.1 of Chapter VIII.
  • (1) When the amendments are being made in the Act for the first time, the long title of the Bill shall be as under :– e.g. “to amend the Maharashtra University Act, 2016.”
  • (2) When the amendments have already been made to the Act and subsequent amendments are being made to the said Act, the long title of the Bill shall be as under:- e.g. “further to amend the Maharashtra University Act, 2016.”.
  • (3) In Consolidated Bill (where amendments are being made to more than one Acts), the long title of the Bill shall be as under:- e.g. (i) “further to amend the Maharashtra State Tax on Professions, Trades, Callings and Employments Act, 1975 and the Maharashtra Value Added Tax Act, 2002.”.
  • (ii) “to amend certain tax laws in operation in the State of Maharashtra.”
  • (4) When the amendments are being made to the Central Acts by the State Legislature, the long title shall be as under:–
  • (a) for first time amendment in Central Act :- “to amend the Bhartiya Nagrik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023, in its application to the State of Maharashtra.”.
  • (b) Subsequent amendments in Central Act:- “ further to amend the Bhartiya Nagrik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023, in its application to the State of Maharashtra.”. Short title of Bill.
  • 11.5.2 (1) The short title of the Bill depends on the subject matter of the Bill, contents of the Bill and duration of legislation, etc.
  • (2) Number to the Amendment to the Bill shall be given on the basis of the short title of the Bill and the year in which the amendments are being made in the Act.
  • (3) When the first amendment is being made to the Act in the year, in the short title of the Bill, it shall be mentioned as “(Amendment) Act, 2025” and not as “(First Amendment) Act, 2025” e.g.- “1. This Act may be called the Maharashtra University (Amendment) Act, 2025.”.
  • (4) When the second time or thereafter (Consequtive) amendment is being made in the Act in the same year, in the short title of the Bill, the amendment number shall be mentioned, as follows :- e.g.- (i) “1. This Act may be called the Maharashtra University (Second Amendment) Act, 2024.”.
  • (ii) “1. This Act may be called the Maharashtra University (Third Amendment) Act, 2024.”.
  • (5) When the amendments are being made for levy of tax and its validation or for levy of tax with retrospective effect, then the short title shall be as follows :- e.g. The Maharashtra Tax Laws (Levy, Amendment and Validation) Act, 2025.
  • (6) When the amendments are being made to the Central Acts by the State Legislature, the short title shall be,- as follows :–– e.g. “The Bhartiya Nagrik Suraksha Sanhita (Maharashtra Amendment) Act, 2025.”.
  • (7) In case of retrospective Amendments in Rules and Notification and its validation Bill, the shor title shall be as follows :-- e.g. “The Maharashtra Slum Areas (Improvement, Clearance and Redevelopment) (Amendment, Re-enactment of Rules and Notification of Apex and other Grievance Redressal Committees and Validation) Act, 2023.”.
  • (8) Temporary period amendments and validation Bill, the short title shall be as follows:- e.g. (i) 1. The Maharashtra Temporary Extension of Period for Submitting Validity Certificate (for certain elections to Village Panchayats , Zilla Parishads and Panchayat Samitis) Act, 2023.”
  • (ii) 1. The Bombay Motor Vehicles (Taxation of Passengers) (Amendment and Validation) Act, 1981.
  • (iii) 1. The Maharashtra Tenancy Law and Maharashtra Land Revenue Code (Amendment and Validation of Appointments of Certain Officers and Proceedings) Act, 1981.
  • (9) The above principles shall also be applicable for making amendments in Act by way of Ordinance.
  • (10) In the Ordinance conversion Bill, irrespective of amendment number of Ordinance, the amendment number to the Bill shall be given on the basis of amendments made to the Act, in the year. Example:- The short title of the Ordinance is – “The Maharashtra University (Amendment) Ordinance, 2024.”, the short title of the Ordinance conversion Bill should be same, however, where the Bill to make amendments in the University Act is pending in the House or the Act of such amendments has been published in the Official Gazette, in the same year, in such cases, the short title of the Ordinance conversion Bill will be- “The Maharashtra University (Second Amendment) Act, 2024.”
  • (11) (a) In Consolidated Bill (where amendments are being made to more than one Acts), in such cases, in the short title, the name of the relevant Acts shall be mentioned. e.g. “The Mumbai Municipal Corporation, Maharashtra Education and Employment Guarantee (Cess) and Maharashtra (Urban Areas) Protection and Preservation of Trees (Amendment) Act, 2022”.
  • (b) If the amendments are being made in similar nature of Acts, the short title to the Bill can be given as follows:- e.g. The Tax Laws (Amendment) Act, 2024. The Labour Laws (Amendment) Act, 2024. The Criminal Laws (Amendment) Act, 2024.
  • (12) Where the amendments have been made in any Act or are being made in a Consolidated Bill/Act and in separate Bill, any amendments are being made in any Act mentioned in Consolidated Bill/Act, by way of new Bill, in such cases, the amendment made by new Bill shall be considered as different amendment Bill and the number of amendment shall be given accordingly. e.g. The short title of the Consolidated Bill/Act shall be- “The Mumbai Municipal Corporation, Maharashtra Education and Employment Guarantee (Cess) and Maharashtra (Urban Areas) Protection and Preservation of Trees (Amendment) Act, 2022” and the other amendments are being made in one Act i.e. in the Mumbai Municipal Corporation Act, by another Bill in the same year, then the short title of the said New Bill shall be,– The Mumbai Municipal Corporation (Amendment) Act, 2022.
  • (13) While making amendments in any Act, the standard formats of Amendments given in Annexure I shall be followed.
  • (14) When the amendments are being made in Rules with retrospective effect and the Act do not empower to make amendments with retrospective effect, in such cases, the amendments should be made only by making legislation. The same principles shall be applicable for making amendments in Notification (see Annexure I).
  • (15) While making retrospective amendments, it shall be considered whether the validity provisions should be made or not. Arrangement of provisions.
  • 11.6 The provisions of draft of Principal Legislation shall be arranged in the following manner :–
  • (i) the material should be arranged in logical order;
  • (ii) general provisions should be followed by specific provisions and exceptions;
  • (iii) provisions that relate to the same subject should be grouped together;
  • (iv) provisions should be arranged in temporal sequence;
  • (v) provisions that are significant should precede provisions of lesser importance;
  • (vi) sections should be limited in the number or subsections they contain and as a general rule section should not have more than six sub-sections;
  • (vii) Act should be divided into group of sections under heading; and
  • (viii) Sections should be numbered. Sequence of provisions.

11.7

While drafting the Principal Legislation, the provisions of the Bill shall be arranged in the following sequence:-

  • (1) The provisions of an Act must be arranged in a logical order.
  • (2) The parts of an Act are normally arranged in the following order :-
  • (a) Long title;
  • (b) Preamble;
  • (c) Short title, extent and commencement;
  • (d) Provisions regarding application of Act;
  • (e) Definitions;
  • (f) Provisions of the Act beginning with the general principles followed by the exceptions;
  • (g) Implementing authorities and powers;
  • (h) Penalty provisions;
  • (i) Transitional provisions;
  • (j) Power to make Rules/Regulations/By-laws, if necessary;
  • (k) Consequential amendments, including repeals;
  • (l) Schedules;
  • (m) Forms.
  • (3) The choice of names for the parts of the structure is a matter of convention. Check List. 11.8 When the draft is prepared, the draftsman should check the following :––
  • (1) Does the long title covers all important subjects of the legislation for which the law is proposed to be enacted ;
  • (2) Check the short title ending with the year in which Bill is to be introduced ;
  • (3) Check the amendment number ;
  • (4) Check the enacting formula of the Bill ;
  • (5) Check for essential parts of the Bill, such as the long title, short title, extent and commencement, definitions, etc ;
  • (6) Check whether terms used in the text are defined in the definition clause ;
  • (7) The statutory references used in the Bill are correct and accurate ;
  • (8) Does the citation of the Acts referred in the Act are accurate in the margin against the names of Act ;
  • (9) Does the cross references of sections are accurate ;
  • (10) Does the contents of the Bill follow in logical sequence ;
  • (11) Check for omissions, spelling errors and typographical errors ;
  • (12) Check section numbers, sub-sections and clauses are properly numbered ;
  • (13) Check Chapters and sections are properly arranged ; H 2316-15
  • (14) Check the Statement of Objects and Reasons are appended to the Bill ;
  • (15) Does the Memorandum Regarding Delegated Legislation required to the Bill ;
  • (16) Does the Financial Memorandum is required to the Bill.
